The theoretically largest decay is
\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\rho(770)\,\rho(770)
, which has been experimentally seen. Yet, the decay width of about
108\,\,
MeV is surely too large, since using a value close to this upper limit would imply that the the sum of all the decays of the state
\rho_{3}(1690)
would overshoot the experimental total width of
(161\pm 10)\,\,
MeV. This is in agreement with the ratio

\displaystyle\frac{\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\rho(770)\,\rho(770)}}{\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\pi^{\pm}\,\pi^{+}\,\pi^{-}\,\pi^{0}}}\,,

(76)

which is smaller than
1
for all experimental measurements
Baltay et al. 1978
;
Bartsch et al. 1970
;
Kliger et al. 1974
;
Thompson et al. 1974
, which are listed by the PDG
Zyla et al. 2020
.

However, we can then obtain a second, more realistic estimate of these decay channels by summing up the largest decay channels:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rho_{3}}^{\text{tot}}=161\,\,\text{MeV}\approx\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(77)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\pi\,\pi}+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\bar{K}\,K}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\rho(770)\,\eta}+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\bar{K}^{\ast}(892)\,K}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\omega(782)\,\pi}+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\phi(1020)\,\pi}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow a_{2}(1320)\,\pi}+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow a_{1}(1260)\,\pi}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow h_{1}(1170)\,\pi}+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow h_{1}(1415)\,\pi}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\rho(770)\,\rho(770)}+\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\text{other}}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

where
\Gamma_{\rho_{3}\rightarrow\text{other}}
refers to all other decay channels not listed above. Anyhow, these should be small. For
\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow h_{1}(1170)\,\pi}
we use the smaller value presented in Table
9
, assuming a large mixing
\beta_{b_{1}}=-40^{\circ}
. In this way we estimate the more reasonable upper limit

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow\rho(770)\,\rho(770)}\lesssim 30\,\,\text{MeV}\,,

(78)

from which follows,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}^{2}\lesssim 148\,.

(79)

The corresponding estimates for the other channels can be found in Table
12
.

Following this estimate, we perform the sum of the predicted and sizable decay channels for the other spin-
3
mesons, obtaining:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{sum}}_{K_{3}^{\ast}(1780)}\approx\,

\displaystyle 146\,\,\text{MeV}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(80)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{sum}}_{\omega_{3}(1670)}\approx\,

\displaystyle 175\,\,\text{MeV}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(81)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\mathrm{sum}}_{\phi_{3}(1850)}\approx\,

\displaystyle 80\,\,\text{MeV}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(82)

which are in agreement with the experimental values from the PDG
Zyla et al. 2020
,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{K_{3}^{\ast}(1780)}^{\text{tot}}=\,

\displaystyle(159\pm 21)\,\,\text{MeV}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(83)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\omega_{3}(1670)}^{\text{tot}}=\,

\displaystyle(168\pm 10)\,\,\text{MeV}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(84)

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\phi_{3}(1850)}^{\text{tot}}=\,

\displaystyle(87_{-23}^{+28})\,\,\text{MeV}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(85)

These results demonstrate that the model is internally consistent.

IV
Phenomenology of the
J^{PC}=3^{--}
glueball

In this chapter, we study the branching ratios of the decays of a hypothetical glueball with
J^{PC}=3^{--}
. Similar to all other bound states of gluons, this glueball is not yet experimentally detected. However, lattice QCD calculations in the quenched approximation predict its mass of approximately
4.13\,\,
GeV
Morningstar and Peardon 1999
,
4.33\,\,
GeV
Meyer 2004
, or
4.20\,\,
GeV
Chen et al. 2006
. For general works on glueballs, see
e.g.
Refs.
Gregory et al. 2012
;
Ochs 2013
;
Mathieu et al. 2009
;
Crede and Meyer 2009
. For model approaches to the phenomenology of glueballs, which are similar to this one, see for example previous publications by some of the authors
Giacosa et al. 2005a
;
Koenigstein and Giacosa 2016
;
Janowski et al. 2014
;
Giacosa et al. 2017
;
Eshraim et al. 2013
.

Before we start our discussion, we explicitly state that all branching ratios should be considered as first indicative results.

IV.1
The effective model for glueball decays

The Lagrangian describing the decays of this glueball can be obtained by making use of the fact that each glueball is a flavor singlet – an object, which is invariant under
SU_{\mathrm{V}}(3)
transformations. Hence, we can perform the simple replacement

\displaystyle W^{\mu\nu\rho}\rightarrow G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\cdot\openone_{3\times 3}

(86)

in the effective interaction terms, where
\openone_{3\times 3}
is the flavor-space-identity matrix and
G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}
is the glueball field
Giacosa et al. 2005a
;
Eshraim et al. 2013
. As a consequence, the interaction terms that come with a commutator vanish and the only nonzero contributions to a residual IR-effective action for the
3^{--}
glueball can be derived from the former interaction terms involving the anticommutator. Thus,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{G_{3}(4200)\rightarrow V_{1}+P}\neq 0\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma_{G_{3}(4200)\rightarrow B_{1}+P}\neq 0\,.

(87)

The corresponding Lagrangian terms are reported in Table
13
.

Table 13:
Strong interaction Lagrangian terms for the hypothetical glueball state
G_{3}(4200)
[in comparison with Eq. (
22
)].

decay mode

interaction Lagrangians

G_{3}\rightarrow V_{1}+P

\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}v_{1}p}=c_{g_{3}v_{1}p}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{(\partial_{\nu}V_{1,\rho}),\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}P)\big\}_{+}\big]

G_{3}\rightarrow V_{1}+A_{1}

\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}=c_{g_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{(\partial_{\nu}V_{1,\rho}),\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}A_{1,\sigma})\big\}_{+}\big]

G_{3}\rightarrow B_{1}+P

\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}b_{1}p}=c_{g_{3}b_{1}p}\,G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{B_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\big\}_{+}\big]

G_{3}\rightarrow B_{1}+A_{1}

\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}=c_{g_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}\,G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{B_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}A_{1,\rho})\big\}_{+}\big]

In addition, there are also decays of the type
G_{3}(4200)\rightarrow V_{1}+A_{1}
and
G_{3}(4200)\rightarrow B_{1}+A_{1}
that were not included in for the lightest conventional mesons with
J^{PC}=3^{--}
because they were kinematically forbidden. They are however possible for the glueball
G_{3}(4200)
since it is expected to be much heavier than the
3^{--}
nonet (
6
). Below we present the theoretical predictions for ratios of decays, which can be easily calculated following the same steps of the previous sections. The only ingredients that are needed for a determination of the branching ratios are the masses of the particles, which are for the sake of simplicity again assumed to be exact. For the
3^{--}
glueball mass, we use
m_{g_{3}}=4.2\,\,
GeV
Chen et al. 2006
.

Decay process
G_{3}\rightarrow V_{1}+P

The interaction Lagrangian for decaying spin-
3
tensor glueball into a vector and a pseudoscalar meson reads

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}v_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(88)

\displaystyle=\,

An extended version is given by Eq. (

) in App.

. The corresponding decay formula is analogous to Eq. (

),

\displaystyle\Gamma_{G_{3}\rightarrow V_{1}+P}(m_{g_{3}},m_{v_{1}},m_{p})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

(89)

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}v_{1}p}^{2}\,\frac{\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1},p}\big|^{7}}{105\uppi}\,\kappa_{i}\,\Theta(m_{g_{3}}-m_{v_{1}}-m_{p})\,.\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

Using vector meson dominance via the shift of Eq. (

) and analogous replacements, we can also construct a radioactive decay term for the spin-

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}\gamma p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(90)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}\gamma p}\,\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,(\partial_{\nu}a_{\rho})\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{Q,\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}P)\big\}_{+}\big]\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

An extended version of this Lagrangian can be found in Eq. (

) in App.

. For the decay formula one derives

\displaystyle\Gamma_{G_{3}\rightarrow\gamma+P}(m_{g_{3}},m_{p})=\,

where the corresponding factors
\kappa_{i}^{\gamma}
are listed in Table
26
. Similar to Eq. (

m_{a}
and

in Eq. (

V
Discussion of the construction of the model

In this section we discuss the interpretation of Eq. (
22
), the interaction Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{W,\text{total}}
, which was constructed by keeping only the lowest possible number of derivatives for a given interaction term. We justify this choice by following two different lines of argumentation. First, our effective model naturally emerges, if the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{W,\text{total}}
is interpreted as being part of a more general and complete chiral hadronic model in the vacuum, such as the (extended) linear sigma model
Koch 1997
, which is aforementioned in the introduction. Second, a (Functional) Renormalization Group perspective also indicates that terms with the lowest derivatives might be retained as the dominant contributions within our effective hadronic model.

The eLSM is built under the assumption of chiral symmetry, thus

\displaystyle U_{\mathrm{L}}(3)\times U_{\mathrm{R}}(3)=SU_{\mathrm{L}}(3)\times SU_{\mathrm{R}}(3)\times U_{\mathrm{L}}(1)\times U_{\mathrm{R}}(1)\,,

and the assumption of scale invariance. Besides the small explicit breaking of both of these symmetries, because of the nonzero bare quark masses, which derive from the electro-weak sector, the symmetries are also broken dynamically. The
SU_{\mathrm{L}}(3)\times SU_{\mathrm{R}}(3)
breaks down to a
SU_{\mathrm{V}}(3)
symmetry spontaneously, which was already discussed before. Also the scale invariance breaks down via gluonic quantum fluctuations and the emergence of a gluon condensate, which is also called the
conformal/trace anomaly

Collins et al. 1977
;
Peskin and Schroeder 1995
;
Greiner et al. 2007
.
Additionally, a second anomaly – the chiral anomaly – breaks
U_{\mathrm{A}}(1)
and explains the heavy mass of
\eta^{\prime}(958)
as well as the large pseudoscalar mixing angle
’t Hooft 1986
. As discussed in Ref.
Giacosa et al. 2018
and commented in various part of this work, this anomaly may affect also the masses and the decays of other nonets (those being part of homochiral multiplets). In the chiral limit and ignoring the chiral anomaly all the interaction terms of the eLSM are invariant under scale/dilatation transformations. Consequently, they are parametrized by dimensionless coupling constants.

The Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3},\text{total}}
(
22
), that we use in this work, contains various decay constants, see Table
3
. With the exception of the last entry, all of them are dimensionful. At a first sight, this feature seems in disagreement with the requirement that should descend from a chiral model such as the eLSM
Parganlija et al. 2013
;
Janowski et al. 2014
. Yet, this is not the case and a closer inspection also shows why the lowest number of derivatives should be kept. First, let us recall that the spontaneous symmetry breaking implies a peculiar scalar-axial-vector mixing, that is removed by the a shift of the axial-vector nonet
A_{1}
. In the simplified case with vanishing bare quark masses, where
U_{\mathrm{V}}(3)
is exact (this is enough for our illustrative purposes), it takes the form
Parganlija et al. 2013
;
Eshraim et al. 2020
:

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\mu}\mapsto A_{1}^{\mu}+Z\,w\,\partial^{\mu}P\,,

(97)

where
Z\approx 1.6
and
w\approx\frac{g_{1}\phi_{N}}{m_{a_{1}}^{2}}
where
g_{1}\approx g_{\rho}\approx 5.5
,
m_{a_{1}}\simeq 1.4\,\,
GeV, and
\phi_{N}\approx Zf_{\pi}
(
f_{\pi}=92.4\,\,
MeV is the pion decay constant).

Now, the eLSM was studied in a variety of frameworks: Besides (pseudo)scalar and (axial-)vector mesons
Parganlija et al. 2013
and the aforementioned calculation of glueballs
Janowski et al. 2014
;
Giacosa et al. 2017
;
Eshraim et al. 2013
, it was also applied to study hybrid mesons
Eshraim et al. 2020
and excited scalar mesons
Parganlija and Giacosa 2017
. Even if various mesonic nonets were not included in the eLSM yet, it is clear, which features such terms should have.

Within this framework, the
W_{3}PP
-Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}pp}=g_{w_{3}pp}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[W^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\big[P,\,(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\big]_{-}\big]\,,

(98)

where
g_{w_{3}pp}
has dimension
E^{-2}
and the
W_{3}A_{1}P
-Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{1}p}=g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[W^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\big[A_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\big]_{-}\big]\,,

(99)

where
g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}
has dimension
E^{-1},
can be seen as the result of the interaction Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}=g_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}\,\mathrm{tr}\big(W^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\big[A_{1,\mu},\,\partial_{\nu}A_{1,\rho}\big]_{-}\big)\,,

(100)

involving solely the dimensionless coupling
g_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}
via the shift of Eq. (
97
) applied once/twice. In other words, only the Lagrangian
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}
is part of a generalized eLSM Lagrangian that includes fields with
J=3
, in agreement with dilatation invariance. Then, upon spontaneous symmetry breaking and mixing/shifts, the Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{1}p}
and
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}pp}
are a consequence of
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}
, thus explaining how dimensional couplings appear in the model, even if one starts solely with dimensionless ones.

While the decay
W_{3}\rightarrow A_{1}\,A_{1}
is not kinematically allowed, the corresponding term explains how the first and the fifth entries of Table
3
emerge. The simple relations

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}pp}\approx Z^{2}\,w^{2}\,g_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}

(101)

and

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}\approx Z\,w\,g_{w_{3}a_{1}a_{1}}

(102)

follow. Moreover, the ratio
g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}/g_{w_{3}pp}\approx(Z\,w)^{-1}
, out of which we may estimate that

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}^{2}\approx 3\cdot 10^{-4}\,\,\text{MeV}^{-2}

(103)

is obtained (it should be however stressed that the present heuristic discussion cannot provide a precise determination of ratios of couplings, but it solely offers a guide to understand the origin of the model). This value leads to
\Gamma_{\rho_{3}(1690)\rightarrow a_{1}(1260)\,\pi}\approx 2\,\,
MeV, as previously mentioned.

Next, let us consider the
W_{3}B_{1}P
-Lagrangian,

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}b_{1}p}=g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}\,\mathrm{tr}\big(W^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\big\{B_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\big\}_{+}\big)\,,

(104)

with
g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}
, which can be seen as emerging from

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}=g_{w_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[W^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\big\{B_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}A_{1,\rho})\big\}_{+}\big]\,,

(105)

with a dimensionless coupling
g_{w_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}
. The
A_{1}
-shift introduced above implies that

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}\approx Z\,w\,g_{w_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}\,.

(106)

Next, let us study the remaining terms. The
W_{3}V_{1}V_{1}
-Lagrangian is already dilatation invariant, while the two other Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}v_{1}p}
and
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{2}p}
are not, since they involve the coupling
g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}
and
g_{w_{3}a_{2}p}
, having dimensions
E^{-3}
and
E^{-2}
. In this case, one could build analogous Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}
and
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{2}a_{1}}
, but their coupling coupling constants
g_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}
and
g_{w_{3}a_{2}a_{1}}
still have dimension
E^{-2}
and
E^{-1}
. This is however understandable, since these terms involve the Levi-Civita pseudotensor
\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}
and hence are a manifestation of the the chiral anomaly.

In conclusion, terms being part of the model are (i) dilatation invariant or (ii) can be obtained from dilatation invariant terms of a more general underlying chiral model via the shift of the axial-vector nonet or (iii) they are linked to the chiral anomaly. Now, one also understand why only terms with the lowest number of derivatives appear.

For the effective interaction terms of the
3^{--}
glueball, the argument is completely analogous.

Still, we also provide a brief alternative motivation for the construction of the model from a renormalization group perspective. In this framework, we know that the UV limit of all hadronic physics is QCD, where the degrees of freedom are quarks and gluons. However, we want to describe low-energy QCD and the effective hadronic degrees of freedom. By integrating out quantum fluctuations of gluons and quarks via renormalization group transformations from the UV scales to the IR scales including dynamical hadronization, one finally ends up with an effective hadronic theory, were hadrons are the effective degrees of freedom. While integrating out quantum fluctuations momentum shell after momentum shell, all kinds of effective couplings/vertices, which are in accordance with the UV symmetries of the theory – the symmetries of QCD – will be generated. This process is effectively described as the RG flow of the coupling constants of the theory, where effective couplings of hadrons are initialy zero and only generated dynamically during the RG flow. The same applies to the effective fermionic and hadronic quantum fields.

An efficient and modern framework to describe this process, which effectively is an implementation of Wilsons idea of the RG
Wilson 1971a
;
Wilson 1971b
;
Polchinski 1984
, is the
Functional Renormalization Group
(FRG) in its formulation via the
Exact Renormalization Group Equation

Wetterich 1993
;
Morris 1994
, see for example Refs.
Gies 2012
;
Pawlowski 2007
;
Polonyi 2003
;
Gies and Wetterich 2002
;
Braun et al. 2010
;
Braun et al. 2016
and Ref.
Dupuis et al. 2020
for an comprehensive up-to-date review. For our purpose, it is solely important to note that the FRG is most efficiently formulated on the level of the quantum effective IR action
\Gamma[\Phi]
, which is the generating functional of all
1
PI-
n
-point-correlation functions. The FRG allows to calculate
\Gamma[\Phi]
– at least within certain truncations – from the UV theory, which here is QCD. The interesting part is that the quantum effective IR action contains all kinds of effective vertices, that were generated during the RG flow and contain all information about the higher energy scales. For the calculation of decays
etc.
, however, only these effective couplings are relevant and calculations are performed at tree level, because all loop contributions are already contained in the couplings and because we are working with the
1
PI-
n
-point-correlation functions. Dynamical symmetry breaking is realized in this framework as a nontrivial vacuum/ground-state, which realizes as a minimum of the effective action
\Gamma[\Phi]
. All
1
PI-
n
-point functions must be extracted at this physical point, which yields shifts like in Eq. (
97
).

In practice, it is almost impossible to really do this calculation from first principles, especially concerning couplings for mesons with higher spin and large masses. Nevertheless, we know from the previous argumentation that a low IR-effective action
\Gamma[\Phi]
must contain all possible interaction terms, which are in accordance with the symmetry of the theory and if shifted to the physical point, then it must contain all symmetries of the vacuum of the theory. This also applies to the effective couplings of our spin-
3
mesons. Thus, we can use the following argument: instead of calculating all interaction terms and couplings, which are in accordance with the symmetries of QCD, via an RG flow from first principles (which is anyhow impossible), we just write down all possible effective interaction terms, that are invariant under the residual IR vacuum-symmetries of QCD and simply fit the effective coupling constants to experimental data. This approach additionally justifies the use of tree level-calculations, because experimental measurements for decay widths
etc.
already contain all information about the high energy scales of QCD and so does an effective IR action
\Gamma[\Phi]
, from which information is extracted at tree level. We therefore interpret all effective Lagrangians
\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}\circ\circ}
as being part of an effective IR action
\Gamma[\Phi]
for QCD.

Last, we argue, that as long as the momentum exchange in processes like three-point-interactions (decays) is small, we can expand an effective IR action in powers of momenta of the effective hadronic fields. In the context of the FRG, this approach is denoted as a
derivative expansion
and turned out as a decent approximation in a lot of low-energy models of QCD
Grahl and Rischke 2013
;
Eser et al. 2015
;
Rennecke and Schaefer 2017
;
Pawlowski and Rennecke 2014
;
Eser et al. 2018
;
Divotgey et al. 2019
;
Eser et al. 2020
;
Cichutek et al. 2020
;
Otto et al. 2020a
;
Otto et al. 2020b
;
Jung and von
Smekal 2019
;
Heller and Mitter 2016
.

All in all, both approaches complement each other and explain that we only retain terms that are of the lowest orders in the derivatives of the fields, that are of leading order in a large-
N_{\mathrm{c}}
expansion and that are flavor invariant.

VI
Conclusions

In this paper, we have studied the decays of the lightest mesonic
\bar{q}q
nonet with quantum numbers
J^{PC}=3^{--}
using an effective QFT model based on flavor symmetry. Our model retained only the dominant terms in an large-
N_{\mathrm{c}}
expansion and the lowest possible number of derivatives. By comparing the theoretical results with the current status of experimental data for decay width and some known branching ratios, which are all reported by PDG
Zyla et al. 2020
, we conclude that the
\bar{q}q
assignment works quite well. Still, we remark that there are also other rather successful approaches different from the
\bar{q}q
picture towards a coherent description of the nature of the
J^{PC}=3^{--}
states, see
e.g.
via multi-
\rho(770)
resonances
Roca and Oset 2010
;
Yamagata-Sekihara et al. 2010
. However, also in our work some of the decay channels deserve deeper theoretical and experimental investigation. Additionally, we presented decay ratios of a putative and not yet detected
J^{PC}=3^{--}
glueball by considering decays into the vector-pseudoscalar and pseudovector-pseudoscalar mesonic pairs. In summary, we provide many qualitative predictions of strong and radiative decays of conventional
J^{PC}=3^{--}
mesons and the
J^{PC}=3^{--}
glueball for future experimental tests.

Strong-interaction matter under extreme conditions
– project number 315477589 – TRR 211. A.K. also acknowledges the support of the
Friedrich-Naumann-Foundation for Freedom
, the
Giersch Foundation
and the
Helmholtz Graduate School for Hadron and Ion Research
.

(NCN) through the
OPUS
projects 2019/33/B/ST2/00613 and no. 2018/29/B/ST2/02576.

Appendix A
Masses and strange-nonstrange mixing angles

For a certain nonet, we denote as
\vec{a}
the triplet of fields describing the isospin
I=1
elements
\bar{d}u
,
\bar{u}d
,
{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,(\bar{u}u-\bar{d}d)}
, with
K^{\pm}
the complex kaonic fields representing the doublet
\bar{s}u
and
\bar{u}s
, and
\bar{K}^{0}
the analogous fields
\bar{d}s
and
\bar{s}d
for the neutral kaonic elements, and as
f_{8}
and
f_{0}
the octet and the singlet elements, whose quark-antiquark configurations are
\frac{1}{\sqrt{6}}\,(\bar{u}u+\bar{d}d-2\bar{s}s)
and
\frac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\,(\bar{u}u+\bar{d}d+\bar{s}s)
, respectively. The corresponding
SU_{\mathrm{V}}(3)
-invariant mass term reads (omitting possible Lorentz indices for simplicity),

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}\,m_{a}^{2}\,\vec{a}^{\,2}+m_{K}^{2}\,(K^{+}\,K^{-}+\bar{K}^{0}\,K^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(107)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\,m_{8}^{2}\,f_{8}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,m_{0}^{2}\,f_{0}^{2}+m_{08}\,f_{0}\,f_{8}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

where also a singlet-octet mixing parameter
m_{08}
has been introduced. The masses of the fields
\vec{a}
,
\bar{K}^{0/\pm}
, and
f_{8}
are expressed as

\displaystyle m_{a}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{nonet}}^{2}+2\,\delta_{n}^{2}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(108)

\displaystyle m_{K}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{nonet}}^{2}+\delta_{n}^{2}+\delta_{s}^{2}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(109)

\displaystyle m_{8}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{nonet}}^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}\,\delta_{n}^{2}+\tfrac{4}{3}\,\delta_{s}^{2}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(110)

where
m_{\mathrm{nonet}}
is the mass of the nonet in the chiral limit as a consequence of spontaneous symmetry breaking (see
e.g.
Ref.
Parganlija et al. 2013
), and
\delta_{n}^{2}
represents the (almost negligible) contribution of the bare nonstrange quarks
u
and
d
and
\delta_{s}^{2}
the (on hadronic scales) small but nonnegligible contribution of the bare quark
s
. (Besides the pseudoscalar nonet, one simply approximates
\delta_{n}\propto m_{u}\simeq m_{d}
and
\delta_{s}\propto m_{s}
.) From the expressions above it follows that

\displaystyle m_{8}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle\tfrac{4}{3}\,m_{K}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{3}\,m_{a}^{2}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(111)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{nonet}}^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}\,\delta_{n}^{2}+\tfrac{4}{3}\,\delta_{s}^{2}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

The mass of the singlet member of the nonet may contain an additional unknown contribution (due to the anomaly and/or conversion to gluons), parametrized by:

\displaystyle m_{0}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle m_{\mathrm{nonet}}^{2}+\tfrac{4}{3}\,\delta_{n}^{2}+\tfrac{2}{3}\,\delta_{s}^{2}+\alpha=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(112)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\tfrac{2}{3}\,m_{K}^{2}+\tfrac{1}{3}\,m_{a}^{2}+\alpha\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

Next, we diagonalize the system in the isoscalar sector. We then introduce the physical fields
f
and
f^{\prime}
as

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-f^{\prime}\\
f\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}&-\sin\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\\
\sin\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}&\cos\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}f_{8}\\
f_{0}\end{pmatrix}

(113)

where
\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}
is the corresponding mixing angle,

\displaystyle\tan\big(2\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\big)=\,

\displaystyle\frac{2m_{08}}{m_{0}^{2}-m_{8}^{2}}\,,

(114)

which is obtained via diagonalization of the mass matrix together with the relation,

\displaystyle m_{8}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}\cos^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}+m_{f}^{2}\sin^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(115)

\displaystyle m_{0}^{2}=\,

\displaystyle m_{f}^{2}\cos^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}+m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}\sin^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(116)

In Eq. (
113
) the extra minus sign in front of
f^{\prime}
is introduced for later convenience. Using Eq. (
115
) one gets

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{8}^{2}-3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}}{-3m_{8}^{2}+3m_{f}^{2}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(117)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}\cos^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}+3m_{f}^{2}\sin^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}-3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}}{-3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}\cos^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}-3m_{f}^{2}\sin^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}+3m_{f}^{2}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\tan^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

but on the other hand using the above expressions for the masses the
l.h.s.
can be written as

\displaystyle\frac{3m_{8}^{2}-3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}}{-3m_{8}^{2}+3m_{f}^{2}}=\frac{4m_{K}^{2}-m_{a}^{2}-3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}}{-4m_{K}^{2}+m_{a}+3m_{f}^{2}}\,,

(118)

leading to expressions used in the PDG
Zyla et al. 2020
to calculate the mixing angle in the singlet-octet basis:

\displaystyle\tan^{2}\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}=\frac{4m_{k}^{2}-m_{a}^{2}-3m_{f^{\prime}}^{2}}{-4m_{K}^{2}+m_{a}+3m_{f}^{2}}\,.

(119)

In the last step we relate this to the (for our purposes) more convenient nonstrange-strange basis
f_{N}
and
f_{S}
that corresponds to the configurations
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\,(\bar{u}u+\bar{d}d)
and
\bar{s}s
. Via

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}f_{8}\\
f_{0}\end{pmatrix}=\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}-\sqrt{2}&1\\
1&\sqrt{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{N}\end{pmatrix}\,,

(120)

we introduce the strange-nonstrange mixing
\beta
as:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-f^{\prime}\\
f\end{pmatrix}=

(121)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{\sqrt{3}}\begin{pmatrix}\cos\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}&-\sin\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\\
\sin\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}&\cos\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}-\sqrt{2}&1\\
1&\sqrt{2}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{N}\end{pmatrix}=

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}-\cos\beta&\sin\beta\\
\sin\beta&\cos\beta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}f_{S}\\
f_{N}\end{pmatrix}\,,

or, as reported in the main text, as:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}f\\
f^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\cos\beta&\sin\beta\\
-\sin\beta&\cos\beta\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}f_{N}\\
f_{S}\end{pmatrix}

(122)

The strange-nonstrange mixing angle
\beta
and the angle
\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}
can be obtained by Eq. (
121
) as:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{3}}\cos\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}-\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{3}}\sin\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(123)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\sin\beta\equiv\sin(-\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}+\beta_{0})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

thus

\displaystyle\beta=-\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}+\beta_{0}

(124)

with

\beta_{0}=\arccos\sqrt{\tfrac{2}{3}}\simeq 35.3^{\circ}\,.

(125)

The mixing angle
\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}
(and hence
\beta
) has been calculated in the PDG for certain well-known mesonic nonets (pseudoscalar, vector, as well as
J^{PC}=2^{++}
and
J^{PC}=3^{--}
). While for the pseudoscalar case we employ the result of Ref.
Amelino-Camelia et al. 2010
(where not only masses but also decays are used to get
\beta
), in the remaining three cases we used the value
\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}
to determine the strange-nonstrange mixing angle. With the exception of the peculiar pseudoscalar nonet, where the axial anomaly is large, the other values of
\beta
calculated from
\beta_{\text{\tiny PDG}}
are quite small – in agreement with the theoretical expectations presented in Ref.
Giacosa et al. 2018
. As a consequence, the parameter
\alpha
of Eq. (
112
) is negligible. In particular, for
J=3
the numerical value
\beta=\beta_{w_{3}}=3.5^{\circ}
has been used throughout our calculations. When future experimental data will be more accurate, one could also include
\beta_{w_{3}}
as a fit parameter in an improved model. In this respect, better experimental data for radiative decays would be useful.

Appendix B
Extended form of the Lagrangians of the model

In this appendix, we provide the explicit form of the interaction Lagrangians in Table
3
and in Table
13
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}pp}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(126)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}pp}\,\mathrm{tr}\big(W_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\big[P,\,(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\big]_{-}\big)=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{g_{w_{3}pp}}{4}\,\Big(\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\rho^{0,\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{+\bar{K}^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})-K^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+K^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})-K^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\big[\pi^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{-})-\pi^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{+})\big]\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\rho^{+,\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{\sqrt{2}\,\big[-K^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})+K^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})\big]+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\big[\pi^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})-\pi^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{-})\big]\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+2\,\big[-\pi^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})+\pi^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{+})\big]\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\bar{K}^{\ast 0,\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{+K^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})-\pi^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2}\,\big[-K^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{-})+\pi^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})\big]+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+K^{0}\big[(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta)(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta^{\prime})(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big]-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad-\big[\eta(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\eta^{\prime}(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big](\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+K^{\ast 0,\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{-\bar{K}^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})+\pi^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2}\,\big[K^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{+}-\pi^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})\big]-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad-\bar{K}^{0}\big[(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta)(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta^{\prime})(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big]+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\big[\eta(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\eta^{\prime}(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big](\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+K^{\ast+,\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{+K^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})-\pi^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2}\,\big[\bar{K}^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{-})-\pi^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})\big]-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad-K^{-}\big[(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta)(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta^{\prime})(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big]+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\big[\eta(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\eta^{\prime}(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big](\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+K^{\ast-,\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{-K^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})+\pi^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{2}\,\big[-K^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{+})+\pi^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})\big]+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+K^{+}\big[(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta)(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta^{\prime})(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big]-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad-\big[\eta(-\cos\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{p})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad+\eta^{\prime}(\sin\beta_{p}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{p})\big](\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\omega^{\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{(-\cos\beta_{w_{3}}+\sqrt{2}\sin\beta_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\big[\bar{K}^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})-K^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad-K^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})+K^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})\big]\Big\}+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\phi^{\mu\nu\rho}_{3}\Big\{(\sin\beta_{w_{3}}+\sqrt{2}\cos\beta_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\big[\bar{K}^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})-K^{0}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\qquad-K^{+}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})+K^{-}(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})\big]\Big\}\Big)\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}v_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(127)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[W_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\big\{(\partial_{\nu}V_{1,\rho}),\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}P)\big\}_{+}\big]=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}}{4}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\Big(\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\rho^{0}_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\Big\{-(\partial_{\nu}\bar{K}^{\ast 0}_{\rho})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}K^{0})-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad-(\partial_{\nu}K^{\ast 0}_{\rho})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}\bar{K}^{0})+(\partial_{\nu}K^{\ast+}_{\rho})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}K^{-})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}\rho_{1,\mu}^{-})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}\pi^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[(\partial_{\nu}\omega_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}\eta)+(\partial_{\nu}\phi_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\prime})\big]\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\cos(\beta_{p}-\beta_{v_{1}})\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[(\partial_{\nu}\phi_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}\eta)-(\partial_{\nu}\omega_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}\eta^{\prime})\big]\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sin(\beta_{p}-\beta_{v_{1}})\big\}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}\gamma p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(134)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}v_{1}p}\,\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,(\partial_{\nu}a_{\rho})\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{Q,\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}P)\big\}_{+}\big]=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\frac{c_{g_{3}v_{1}p}}{3}\,\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,(\partial_{\nu}a_{\rho})\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\big\{\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+3\,\pi^{0}+\big[\cos(\beta_{p})-\sqrt{2}\,\sin(\beta_{p})\big]\,\eta-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle-\big[\sin(\beta_{p})+\sqrt{2}\,\cos(\beta_{p})\big]\,\eta^{\prime}\big\}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(135)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{(\partial_{\nu}V_{1,\rho}),\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}A_{1,\sigma})\big\}_{+}\big]=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}\,G_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\big\{\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}\bar{K}^{0}_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}K_{1,A,\sigma}^{0})+(\partial_{\nu}K_{1,\mu}^{0})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\bar{K}_{1,A,\sigma}^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}K_{1,\mu}^{+})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}K_{1,A,\sigma}^{-})+(\partial_{\nu}K_{1,\mu}^{-})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}K_{1,A,\sigma}^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}\rho_{1,\mu}^{0})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}a_{1,\sigma}^{0})+(\partial_{\nu}\rho_{1,\mu}^{+})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}a_{1,\sigma}^{-})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+(\partial_{\nu}\rho_{1,\mu}^{-})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}a_{1,\sigma}^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[(\partial_{\nu}\omega_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}f_{1,\sigma})+(\partial_{\nu}\phi_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}f_{1,\sigma}^{\prime})\big]\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\cos(\beta_{a_{1}}-\beta_{v_{1}})\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[(\partial_{\nu}\phi_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}f_{1,\sigma})-(\partial_{\nu}\omega_{1,\mu})(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}f_{1,\sigma}^{\prime})\big]\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad\times\sin(\beta_{a_{1}}-\beta_{v_{1}})\big\}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}b_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(136)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}b_{1}p}\,G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{B_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\big\}_{+}\big]=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}b_{1}p}\,G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\big\{\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\bar{K}^{0}_{1,B,\mu}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{0})+K_{1,B,\mu}^{0}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\bar{K}^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+K_{1,B,\mu}^{+}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{-})+K_{1,B,\mu}^{-}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}K^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+b_{1,\mu}^{0}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{0})+b_{1,\mu}^{+}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{-})+b_{1,\mu}^{-}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\pi^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[h_{1,\mu}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta)+h_{1,\mu}^{\prime}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta^{\prime})\big]\,\cos(\beta_{p}-\beta_{b_{1}})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[h_{1,\mu}^{\prime}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta)-h_{1,\mu}(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}\eta^{\prime})\big]\,\sin(\beta_{p}-\beta_{b_{1}})\big\}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(137)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}\,G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,\mathrm{tr}\big[\big\{B_{1,\mu},\,(\partial_{\nu}A_{1,\rho})\big\}_{+}\big]=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle c_{g_{3}b_{1}a_{1}}\,G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\big\{\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\bar{K}^{0}_{1,B,\mu}(\partial_{\nu}K_{1,A,\rho}^{0})+K_{1,B,\mu}^{0}(\partial_{\nu}\bar{K}_{1,A,\rho}^{0})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+K_{1,B,\mu}^{+}(\partial_{\nu}K_{1,A,\rho}^{-})+K_{1,B,\mu}^{-}(\partial_{\nu}K_{1,A,\rho}^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+b_{1,\mu}^{0}(\partial_{\nu}a_{1,\rho}^{0})+b_{1,\mu}^{+}(\partial_{\nu}a_{1,\rho}^{-})+b_{1,\mu}^{-}(\partial_{\nu}a_{1,\rho}^{+})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[h_{1,\mu}(\partial_{\nu}f_{1,\rho})+h_{1,\mu}^{\prime}(\partial_{\nu}f_{1,\rho}^{\prime})\big]\,\cos(\beta_{a_{1}}-\beta_{b_{1}})+\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle+\big[h_{1,\mu}^{\prime}(\partial_{\nu}f_{1,\rho})-h_{1,\mu}(\partial_{\nu}f_{1,\rho}^{\prime})\big]\,\sin(\beta_{a_{1}}-\beta_{b_{1}})\big\}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

Appendix D
Unpolarized invariant decay amplitudes

This appendix is devoted to the calculation of the unpolarized invariant decay amplitudes. These form the normal decay amplitudes by multiplication with factors
\kappa_{i}
depending on the specific decay channels. We apply the following Feynman rules for each interaction vertex in order to derive the expressions for the decay amplitudes,

\displaystyle\partial^{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\mathrm{i}k_{\circ}^{\mu}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(138)

\displaystyle V_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(139)

\displaystyle B_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda_{b_{1}},\vec{k}_{b_{1}})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(140)

\displaystyle A_{1}^{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(141)

\displaystyle A_{2}^{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu\nu}(\lambda_{a_{2}},\vec{k}_{a_{2}})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(142)

\displaystyle W_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(143)

\displaystyle G_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{g_{3}},\vec{k}_{g_{3}})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(144)

\displaystyle a^{\mu}

\displaystyle\mapsto\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda_{\gamma},\vec{k}_{\gamma})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(145)

where
\epsilon^{\mu(\nu)(\rho)}
are the polarization vectors (tensors),
\lambda
is the specific polarization
6
6

6

For massive spin-
3
tensor fields
\lambda=-3,\,-2,\,-1,\,0,\,+1,\,+2,\,+3
, for massive spin-2 tensor fields
\lambda=-2,\,-1,\,0,\,+1,\,+2
, whereas for massive vector fields
\lambda=-1,\,0,\,+1
, representing the spin orientations in a magnetic field, or respective degrees of freedom of the massive fields. For massless vector fields (photons) there are only two polarizations
\lambda=1,\,2
.
,
k^{\mu}
is the four momentum, and
\vec{k}
is the three momentum.

1.

For the interaction Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}pp}=g_{w_{3}pp}\,W_{3,\mu\nu\rho}\,P^{(1)}\,\big(\partial^{\mu}\partial^{\nu}\partial^{\rho}P^{(2)}\big)\,,

(146)

which describes decays of massive spin-
3
fields into two massive pseudoscalar fields, the unpolarized invariant decay amplitude has the following form

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}pp}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(147)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}g_{w_{3}pp}\,\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\mu}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\nu}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\rho}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

The square of the amplitude reads

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}pp}|^{2}=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

(148)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}pp}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle\times k_{p^{(2)}}^{\mu}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\nu}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\rho}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\bar{\mu}}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\bar{\nu}}\,k_{p^{(2)}}^{\bar{\rho}}=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}pp}^{2}\,\tfrac{2}{35}\,\big|\vec{k}_{p^{(1)},p^{(2)}}\big|^{6}\,.\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

2.

The unpolarized invariant decay amplitude for the interaction of massive spin-
3
fields, massive vector fields and massive pseudoscalar fields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}v_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(text\bigg)}

(149)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,W_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,(\partial_{\nu}V_{1,\rho})\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}P)\vphantom{\bigg(text\bigg)}

reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}v_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(text\bigg)}

(150)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\bigg(text\bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{v_{1}\nu}\,\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\,k_{p}^{\alpha}\,k_{p}^{\beta}\,k_{p\sigma}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(text\bigg)}

The square of the amplitude is

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}v_{1}p}|^{2}=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

(151)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\varepsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}\bar{\sigma}}\times\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\mu}\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle\times k_{v_{1}\nu}\,k_{v_{1}\bar{\nu}}\sum_{\lambda_{v_{1}}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\times\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle\times k_{p}^{\alpha}\,k_{p}^{\beta}\,k_{p\sigma}\,k_{p}^{\bar{\alpha}}\,k_{p}^{\bar{\beta}}\,k_{p\bar{\sigma}}=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{8}{105}\,\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1},p}\big|^{6}\,m_{w_{3}}^{2}\,.\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}}

3.

For the decays of massive spin-
3
fields into massive pseudoscalar fields and photons, the generic interaction Lagrangian is of the general type

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}\gamma p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(152)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}\,\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,W_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,(\partial_{\nu}a_{\rho})\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}\partial_{\sigma}P)\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

The corresponding decay amplitude reads

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}\gamma p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(153)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}\,\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{\gamma,\nu}\,\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{\gamma},\vec{k}_{\gamma})\,k_{p}^{\alpha}\,k_{p}^{\beta}\,k_{p,\sigma}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

The averaged square of the amplitude is

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}\gamma p}|^{2}=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

(154)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g^{2}_{w_{3}v_{1}p}\,\big(\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\big)^{2}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\varepsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}\bar{\sigma}}\,k_{p}^{\alpha}\,k_{p}^{\beta}\,k_{p,\sigma}\,k_{p}^{\bar{\alpha}}\,k_{p}^{\bar{\beta}}\,k_{p,\bar{\sigma}}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\mu}\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{\gamma,\nu}\,k_{\gamma,\bar{\nu}}\,\sum_{\lambda_{\gamma}=1}^{2}\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{\gamma},\vec{k}_{\gamma})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{\gamma},\vec{k}_{\gamma})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}p}^{2}\,\big(\tfrac{e}{g_{\rho}}\big)^{2}\,\tfrac{8}{105}\,\big|\vec{k}_{\gamma,p}\big|^{6}\,m_{w_{3}}^{2}\,.\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

4.

Decays of massive spin-
3
fields into massive spin-2 tensor and massive pseudoscalar fields is described by the following interaction

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{2}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(155)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{2}p}\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\mathchoice{W^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{3,}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{W^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{3,}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{W^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{3,}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}}{W^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.25003pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.81111pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.72221pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.19876pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.13454pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.23895pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.53441pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.77156pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.97969pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{{3,}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\alpha}{\beta}}}}\,(\partial^{\nu}A_{2}^{\rho\alpha})\,(\partial^{\sigma}\partial^{\beta}P)\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

which leads to the amplitude

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}a_{2}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(156)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}g_{w_{3}a_{2}p}\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\,\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{a_{2}}^{\nu}\,\epsilon^{\rho\gamma}(\lambda_{a_{2}},\vec{k}_{a_{2}})\,k_{p}^{\sigma}\,k_{p\beta}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

Squaring the amplitude, we find,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}a_{2}p}|^{2}=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

(157)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{2}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\,\varepsilon_{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\varepsilon_{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}\bar{\sigma}}\,\eta_{\alpha\gamma}\,\eta_{\bar{\alpha}\bar{\gamma}}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\,\epsilon^{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{a_{2}}=-2}^{2}\epsilon^{\rho\gamma}(\lambda_{a_{2}},\vec{k}_{a_{2}})\,\epsilon^{\bar{\rho}\bar{\gamma}}(\lambda_{a_{2}},\vec{k}_{a_{2}})\,k_{a_{2}}^{\nu}\,k_{a_{2}}^{\bar{\nu}}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{p}^{\sigma}\,k_{p\beta}\,k_{p}^{\bar{\sigma}}\,k_{p\bar{\beta}}=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{2}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{2}{105}\,\big|\vec{k}_{a_{2},p}\big|^{4}\,\frac{m_{w_{3}}^{2}}{m_{a_{2}}^{2}}\,\big(2\,\big|\vec{k}_{a_{2},p}\big|^{2}+7\,m_{a_{2}}^{2}\big)\,.\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

5.

The amplitudes for the decays of massive spin-
3
fields into massive pseudoscalar and massive axial-/pseudovector fields, which are described via the interaction Lagrangians

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}b_{1}p}=g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}\,W_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,B_{1,\mu}\,(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)

(158)

and

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}a_{1}p}=g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}\,W_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,A_{1,\mu}\,(\partial_{\nu}\partial_{\rho}P)\,,

(159)

have the same shape,

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}b_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(160)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda_{b_{1}},\vec{k}_{b_{1}})\,k_{p\nu}\,k_{p\rho}\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}a_{1}p}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(161)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})\,k_{p\nu}\,k_{p\rho}\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

Their squares have the following form,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}b_{1}p}|^{2}=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

(162)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\,k_{p\nu}\,k_{p\rho}\,k_{p\bar{\nu}}\,k_{p\bar{\rho}}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{b_{1}}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda_{b_{1}},\vec{k}_{b_{1}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\mu}}(\lambda_{b_{1}},\vec{k}_{b_{1}})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}b_{1}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{2}{105}\,\big|\vec{k}_{b_{1},p}\big|^{4}\,\Bigg(7+3\,\frac{\big|\vec{k}_{b_{1},p}\big|^{2}}{m_{b_{1}}^{2}}\Bigg)\,,\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}a_{1}p}|^{2}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(163)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\,k_{p\nu}\,k_{p\rho}\,k_{p\bar{\nu}}\,k_{p\bar{\rho}}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{a_{1}}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\mu}}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}a_{1}p}^{2}\,\tfrac{2}{105}\,\big|\vec{k}_{a_{1},p}\big|^{4}\,\Bigg(7+3\,\frac{\big|\vec{k}_{a_{1},p}\big|^{2}}{m_{a_{1}}^{2}}\Bigg)\,.\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

6.

The interaction Lagrangian which describes the decay of massive spin-
3
fields into two massive vector fields

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}=g_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}\,W_{3}^{\mu\nu\rho}\,(\partial_{\mu}V_{1,\nu}^{(1)})\,V_{1,\rho}^{(2)}

(164)

leads to the following amplitude

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(165)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}g_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,k_{v_{1}\mu}^{(1)}\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(1)},\vec{k}_{v_{1}}^{(1)})\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(2)},k_{v_{2}}^{(2)})\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

The square of this amplitude is given by

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}|^{2}=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

(166)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\,\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{v_{1}\mu}^{(1)}\,k_{v_{1}\bar{\mu}}^{(1)}\sum_{\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(1)}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(1)},\vec{k}_{v_{1}}^{(1)})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\nu}}(\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(1)},\vec{k}_{v_{1}}^{(1)})\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(2)}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(2)},\vec{k}_{v_{1}}^{(2)})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{v_{1}}^{(2)},\vec{k}_{v_{1}}^{(2)})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}v_{1}}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{105}\,\big(m_{v_{1}^{(1)}}^{2}\,m_{v_{1}^{(2)}}^{2}\big)^{-1}\,\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1}^{(1)},v_{1}^{(2)}}\big|^{2}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\big[6\,\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1}^{(1)},v_{1}^{(2)}}\big|^{4}+35\,m_{v_{1}^{(1)}}^{2}\,m_{v_{1}^{(2)}}^{2}+\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\quad+14\,\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1}^{(1)},v_{1}^{(2)}}\big|^{2}\,\big(m_{v_{1}^{(1)}}^{2}+m_{v_{1}^{(2)}}^{2}\big)\big]\,.\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

7.

For the decays of massive spin-
3
fields into massive vector and axial-vector fields, we use the following Lagrangian

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(167)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,W_{3,\mu\alpha\beta}\,(\partial_{\nu}V_{1,\rho})\,(\partial^{\alpha}\partial^{\beta}A_{1,\sigma})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

to obtain the amplitude

\displaystyle\mathrm{i}\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}=\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(168)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\mathrm{i}g_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,k_{v_{1}\nu}\,\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\times\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{a_{1}}^{\alpha}\,k_{a_{1}}^{\beta}\,\epsilon_{\sigma}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

The square of the amplitude is

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{7}\,|\mathcal{M}_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}|^{2}=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

(169)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}^{2}\,\tfrac{1}{7}\,\varepsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}\,\varepsilon^{\bar{\mu}\bar{\nu}\bar{\rho}\bar{\sigma}}\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times\sum_{\lambda_{w_{3}}=-3}^{+3}\,\epsilon_{\mu\alpha\beta}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\mu}\bar{\alpha}\bar{\beta}}(\lambda_{w_{3}},\vec{k}_{w_{3}})\times\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{v_{1}\nu}\,k_{v_{1}\bar{\nu}}\,\sum_{\lambda_{v_{1}}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\rho}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\rho}}(\lambda_{v_{1}},\vec{k}_{v_{1}})\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle\times k_{a_{1}}^{\alpha}\,k_{a_{1}}^{\beta}\,k_{a_{1}}^{\bar{\alpha}}\,k_{a_{1}}^{\bar{\beta}}\,\sum_{\lambda_{a_{1}}=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\sigma}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})\,\epsilon_{\bar{\sigma}}(\lambda_{a_{1}},\vec{k}_{a_{1}})=\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle g_{w_{3}v_{1}a_{1}}^{2}\,\tfrac{4}{105}\,\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1},a_{1}}\big|^{4}\,\bigg[\big|\vec{k}_{v_{1},a_{1}}\big|^{2}\,\bigg(3+2\,\frac{m_{w_{2}}^{2}}{m_{a_{1}}^{2}}\bigg)+7\,m_{v_{1}}^{2}\bigg]\,.\vphantom{\Bigg(\Bigg)}

We remark that the form of the amplitudes
\mathcal{M}
of most of the decay channels was already derived in Ref.
Wang et al. 2017a
. In contrast to Ref.
Wang et al. 2017a
we neglect higher order derivative couplings and purely rely on the lowest order contribution in derivatives of the fields. Furthermore, we do not include form factors.

In order to obtain the results of this appendix one has to average over all incoming spin polarizations, sum up all possible outgoing polarizations and consider the following completeness relations for massive particles, which are discussed in the subsequent appendix,

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})=-G_{\mu\nu}\,,\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

(170)

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-2}^{+2}\epsilon_{\mu\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta}(\lambda,\vec{k})=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

(171)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{3}\,G_{\mu\nu}\,G_{\alpha\beta}+\tfrac{1}{2}\,(G_{\mu\alpha}\,G_{\nu\beta}+G_{\mu\beta}\,G_{\nu\alpha})\,,\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}\epsilon_{\mu\nu\rho}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\alpha\beta\gamma}(\lambda,\vec{k})=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

(172)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{15}\,\big[G_{\mu\nu}\,(G_{\rho\alpha}\,G_{\beta\gamma}+G_{\rho\beta}\,G_{\alpha\gamma}+G_{\rho\gamma}\,G_{\alpha\beta})+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle+G_{\mu\rho}\,(G_{\nu\alpha}\,G_{\beta\gamma}+G_{\nu\beta}\,G_{\alpha\gamma}+G_{\nu\gamma}\,G_{\alpha\beta})+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle+G_{\nu\rho}\,(G_{\mu\alpha}\,G_{\beta\gamma}+G_{\mu\beta}\,G_{\alpha\gamma}+G_{\mu\gamma}\,G_{\alpha\beta})\big]-\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{6}\,\big[G_{\mu\alpha}\,(G_{\nu\beta}\,G_{\rho\gamma}+G_{\nu\gamma}\,G_{\rho\beta})+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle+G_{\mu\beta}\,(G_{\nu\alpha}\,G_{\rho\gamma}+G_{\nu\gamma}\,G_{\rho\alpha})+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

\displaystyle+G_{\mu\gamma}\,(G_{\nu\alpha}\,G_{\rho\beta}+G_{\nu\beta}\,G_{\rho\alpha})\big]\,,\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-3}^{+3}}

where

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}\equiv\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k_{\mu}k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle k^{2}=m^{2}\,,

(173)

and
(\eta_{\mu\nu})=\mathrm{diag}(+1,-1,-1,-1)
.

For the square of the amplitudes of the radioactive decays in Eq. (
154
) the following completeness relation for photons is used
Greiner and Reinhardt 1996
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})=\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2}}

(174)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle-\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k_{\mu}k_{\nu}}{(k\cdot n)^{2}}+\frac{k_{\mu}n_{\nu}+k_{\nu}n_{\mu}}{(k\cdot n)}\,.\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2}}

Here,
n=(1,0,0,0)^{T}
is the unit vector in temporal direction and
k
is the four momentum of the outgoing photon.

The summations over Minkwoski spacetime indices in the squares of the amplitudes were performed on a symbolic level with
Wolfram Mathematica 12.1

Wolfram Research 2020
using
Sum
and
ParallelSum
. We work in the center of mass frame of the decaying particle. In order to reduce the number of addends in the sums drastically,
w.l.o.g.
we choose the three momenta of the outgoing particles in
z
direction.

Appendix E
Completeness relations

In this appendix, we present how the completeness relations for the polarization vectors and tensors of massive higher-spin fields, like Eqs. (
170
), (
171
) and (
172
), are constructed. These completeness relations are used for the calculation of the unpolarized decay amplitudes in the previous App.
D
.

We begin by deriving the completeness relation for massive spin-1 fields explicitly. This derivation is based on the discussion in Ref.
Greiner and Reinhardt 1996
and presented here for reasons of consistency. Starting from this result for spin-1 fields, we generalize the procedure and show, how the completeness relations for massive higher-spin fields can be constructed. We then provide an explicit derivation of the completeness relations for massive spin-
3
tensor fields. We close the discussion, by elaborating on the degrees of freedom of massive fields of arbitrary integer spin-
J
and their description via rank-
J
tensor fields.

None of the results in this appendix is original to our work. However, we think that the discussion might still be useful for a nonexpert reader.

E.1
Spin-1 fields

The construction of the completeness relation for the polarization vectors
\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})
of the massive (real) vector fields
V^{\mu}(x)
is based on the field equations that describe free massive vector particles.
7
7

7

For free massive axial- and pseudovector fields the construction is identical.
These field equations are given by the Proca equations, which are the Klein-Gordon (KG) equation and a transversality condition (which is explicitly not a gauge),

\displaystyle 0=(\Box+m^{2})\,V^{\mu}(x)\,,

\displaystyle 0=\partial_{\mu}V^{\mu}(x)\,.

(175)

For neutral massive vector mesons, the solutions to the KG equation are plane waves,

\displaystyle V^{\mu}(x)=\int\frac{\mathrm{d}^{3}k}{(2\uppi)^{3}}\frac{1}{2E(\vec{k})}\,\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\Big\{a(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\mathrm{e}^{+\mathrm{i}[E(\vec{k})\,t-\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}]}+a^{\ast}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\mathrm{e}^{-\mathrm{i}[E(\vec{k})\,t+\vec{k}\cdot\vec{x}]}\Big\}\,,

(176)

where
a(\lambda,\vec{k})=a^{\ast}(\lambda,-\vec{k})
are the Fourier amplitudes and
E^{2}(\vec{k})=\vec{k}^{\,2}+m^{2}
is the square of the energy. The transversality condition further implies that the polarization vectors
\epsilon(\lambda,\vec{k})
have to be transversal to the four momentum
k=(E(\vec{k}),\vec{k})^{T}
of the the fields,

\displaystyle 0=k_{\mu}\,\epsilon^{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,.

(177)

From this equation, we can construct the completeness relation for the polarization vectors.

We start with the most general ansatz for a dimensionless rank-two tensor that only depends on the four momentum
k=(E(\vec{k}),\vec{k})^{T}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})=a\,\eta_{\mu\nu}+b\,\frac{k_{\mu}\,k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\,,

(178)

where
a
and
b
are dimensionless constants. The transversality condition (
177
) implies

\displaystyle 0\overset{!}{=}k^{\mu}\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})=k_{\nu}\,(a+b)\,,

(179)

which simplifies our ansatz to

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})=a\,\bigg(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k_{\mu}\,k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\bigg)\,.

(180)

The coefficient
a
can be fixed by choosing an appropriate normalization condition. This is done by demanding that in the rest frame
k=(m,\vec{0})^{T}
for the spatial components the completeness relation should reduce to the Kronecker delta, to have a three-dimensional orthonormal basis of polarization vectors.

\displaystyle\delta_{ij}\overset{!}{=}\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{i}(\lambda,\vec{0})\,\epsilon_{j}(\lambda,\vec{0})=-a\,\delta_{ij}\,.

(181)

We obtain the completeness relation

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}\epsilon_{\mu}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\nu}(\lambda,\vec{k})=\,

\displaystyle-\bigg(\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k_{\mu}\,k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\bigg)\equiv

(182)

\displaystyle\equiv\,

\displaystyle-G_{\mu\nu}\,,\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=-1}^{+1}}

and define the projector
G_{\mu\nu}
, which projects on the subspace of the Minkowski space that is transversal to the four momentum vector
k=(E(\vec{k}),\vec{k})^{T}
of the massive spin-1 field.

E.2
Higher-spin fields

For all kind of higher-spin tensor fields, we proceed analogously. The starting point are the so called Fierz-Pauli equations for a massive field with integer spin
J
. The field is described by a rank-
J
tensor field that fulfills the following equations (see also Refs.
Koenigstein et al. 2016
;
Greiner and Reinhardt 1996
;
Fierz 1939
;
Fierz and Pauli 1939
;
Huang et al. 2002
;
Bouatta et al. 2004
;
Barut and Raczka 1986
for more details on wave equations and degrees of freedom of higher-spin fields),

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle(\Box+m^{2})\,\varphi^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(x)\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(183)

and
\forall\,1\leq m\neq n\leq J
,

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(x)-\varphi^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(x)\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(184)

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha_{m}\alpha_{n}}\,\varphi^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(x)\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(185)

and
\forall\,1\leq m\leq J
,

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\partial_{\alpha_{m}}\varphi^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(x)\,.\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(186)

These equations reduce the
4^{J}
degrees of freedom of a general rank-
J
tensor field in 4-dimensional Minkowski space to the
(2J+1)
degrees of freedom that are necessary to describe a massive spin-
J
field. (We will come back to this point at the end of this appendix.) Note that the Proca equations (
175
) for spin-1 fields are merely a special case of the Fierz-Pauli equations (
183
) - (
186
).

Completely analogous to the vector field, we can thus find a solution to the KG equation and derive the following constraints for the corresponding polarization tensors,
\forall\,1\leq m\neq n\leq J\,,

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})-\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(187)

\displaystyle-\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\eta_{\alpha_{m}\alpha_{n}}\,\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{n}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(188)

\forall\,1\leq m\leq J\,,

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle k_{\alpha_{m}}\epsilon^{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{m}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,,

(189)

Next, to find the corresponding completeness relation, one has to start with an appropriate ansatz for

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-J}^{+J}\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})=\ldots\,.

(190)

Consequently, one should write down the most general rank-
2J
tensor, which only depends on the four momentum
k=(E(\vec{k}),\vec{k})^{T}
and the metric tensor. This means that all possible combinations of
\eta_{\mu\nu}
and
\frac{k_{\mu}k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}
, with all possible permutations of the indices
\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{J}
,
\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{J}
of the polarization tensors have to be included,

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda=-J}^{J}\epsilon_{\alpha_{1}\cdots\alpha_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,\epsilon_{\beta_{1}\cdots\beta_{J}}(\lambda,\vec{k})=

(191)

\displaystyle=\,

\displaystyle+a_{1}\,\eta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots\eta_{\beta_{J-1}\beta_{J}}+\text{all permutations}+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2J+1}}

\displaystyle+b_{1}\,\eta_{\alpha_{1}\alpha_{2}}\cdots\frac{k_{\beta_{J-1}}k_{\beta_{J}}}{k^{2}}+\text{all permutations}+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2J+1}}

\displaystyle+\ldots+\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2J+1}}

\displaystyle+z_{1}\,\frac{k_{\alpha_{1}}k_{\alpha_{2}}}{k^{2}}\cdots\frac{k_{\beta_{J-1}}k_{\beta_{J}}}{k^{2}}+\text{all permutations}\,.\vphantom{\sum_{\lambda=1}^{2J+1}}

Here,
a_{i}
,
b_{i}
,
\ldots
,
z_{i}
stand for the various dimensionless coefficients of the ansatz. An alternative approach is to write down the ansatz for the rank-
2J
tensor on the r.h.s. of the above equation in terms of projection operators

\displaystyle G_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-\frac{k_{\mu}k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle P_{\mu\nu}=\frac{k_{\mu}k_{\nu}}{k^{2}}\,,

(192)

which also form a complete basis.

What follows is identical for both approaches:

To determine the various coefficients, one has to use Eqs. (
187
), (
188
) and (
189
) (or combinations of these constraints) successively until one reaches the point, where only one overall coefficient/factor is left, see for example Ref.
Koenigstein et al. 2016
for massive spin-2 fields. The leftover constant can only be fixed by appropriate normalization. For most problems, a normalization, which produces an orthonormal basis of polarization tensors in the rest frame, like for the vectors in Eq. (
181
), is suitable.

Following these procedures, one eventually arrives at the completeness relations (
171
) and (
172
) for the polarization tensors of spin-2 and spin-
3
fields.

We remark, that an expression for arbitrary spin fields was already presented in Refs.
Harnad 1972
;
Durand 1967
. The expressions for spin-2 and spin-
3
were already presented among others in Refs.
Wang et al. 2017a
;
Wang et al. 2017b
;
Bergstrom et al. 1991
;
Koenigstein et al. 2016
;
Zee 2010
.

E.3
Spin-3 fields revisited

Here, we explicitly present the derivation of the completeness relation for massive spin-
3
fields.

We start by adopting the constraints (
187
), (
188
) and (
189
) to the polarization tensors of rank-3 tensor fields. Thus, the polarization tensors of spin-
3
fields have to fulfill the following conditions,

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{(\mu\nu\rho)}(\lambda,\vec{k})\,,\vphantom{\bigg(\bigg)}

(193)

\displaystyle 0=\,

\displaystyle\mathchoice{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.00928pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.42052pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.72893pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\epsilon^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.95248pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.10892pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{\mu}{\nu}}}_{{{\mu}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.86232pt][c]{$\t
